is obtained from the relation
f=e-Ts
and Eqs.
261
and
262
. In this manuscript we consider for simplicity the isothermal case.

Considering a surface
\mathcal{S}
enclosed by a contour
\mathcal{C}
, moving with the velocity field
\mathbf{v}
and such that the contour
\mathcal{C}
follows the flow, the rate of change of free energy
F=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dSf
is then

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dt}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS[J_{n}^{f}-T\theta]-\int_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}j_{f}^{i}~.

(264)

Appendix E
Virtual work

In this Appendix we derive an alternative expression for the infinitesimal virtual work defined in Eq.
27

salbreux2017activesurfaces
. Using the force and torque balance equations
21
and
22
, the virtual work defined in Eq.
27
can be re-written

\displaystyle dW=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[\mathbf{t}^{i}\cdot d\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{m}^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+(\mathbf{t}^{i}\times\mathbf{e}_{i})\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[t^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+\mathbf{m}^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]~,

(265)

where Eq.
211
has been used to replace d
\mathbf{e}_{i}
in the first line.
By re-expressing
(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{j}
with the aid of Eq.
214
one then finds:

\displaystyle\delta W=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[\overline{t}^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+\overline{m}^{ij}DC_{ij}+m^{i}_{n}(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\cdot\mathbf{n}\right]\,,

(266)

where we use the modified tensors introduced in Eqs.
31
and
32
.

We note that the definition of the virtual work differential
27
allows for a redefinition of the tension and moment tensors
\mathbf{t}^{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{t}^{i}+\epsilon^{ik}\partial_{k}(A\mathbf{n})
,
\mathbf{m}^{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}^{i}+Ag^{ij}\mathbf{e}_{j}
with an arbitrary scalar field
A
, which leaves the virtual work differential invariant. Indeed for any scalar field
A
:

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\left(\mathbf{t}^{i}\cdot d\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{m}^{i}\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right)=\hskip 113.81102pt

\displaystyle\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\left([\mathbf{t}^{i}+\epsilon^{ik}\partial_{k}(A\mathbf{n})]\cdot d\mathbf{X}+(\mathbf{m}^{i}+Ag^{ij}\mathbf{e}_{j})\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right)~,

(267)

which can be proven using using that the difference
\Delta
between the right-hand side and left-hand side in the equation above is:

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}[\partial_{k}(A\mathbf{n})\cdot d\mathbf{X}\epsilon^{ik}+Ad\theta^{i}]

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}[\partial_{k}(A\mathbf{n})\cdot d\mathbf{X}+A\mathbf{n}\cdot\partial_{k}d\mathbf{X}]\epsilon^{ik}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\partial_{k}(AdX_{n})\epsilon^{ik}

\displaystyle=\oint_{\mathcal{S}}dS\nabla_{i}(\partial_{k}(AdX_{n}))\epsilon^{ik}

\displaystyle=0~,

(268)

where we have used Eq.
210
, the divergence theorem
184
, and the commutation relation
193
.

The transformation
\mathbf{m}^{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{m}^{i}+Ag^{ij}\mathbf{e}_{j}
corresponds to
\bar{m}^{ij}\rightarrow\bar{m}^{ij}-A\epsilon^{ij}
, using the definition for the modified moment tensor, Eq.
32
. Choosing
A
to be equal to the antisymmetric part of the tensor
\bar{m}^{ij}
,
A=\frac{1}{2}\bar{m}^{ij}\epsilon_{ij}
, then implies that
\bar{m}^{ij}
can always be symmetrised at the cost of a redefinition of the tension tensor
\mathbf{t}^{i}
.

Appendix F
Translation and rotation invariance

In this Appendix we obtain Gibbs-Duhem relation for polar and nematic surfaces, which result from the invariance of the surface free energy under solid translations and rotations.

F.1
Polar surface

F.1.1
Invariance by translation

To express invariance by translation for a polar surface, we consider the free energy density
f_{0}(c^{\alpha},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{p},\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})
introduced in Eq.
34
with its differential given in Eq.
35
. Because the free energy density
f_{0}
does not depend on the surface position
\mathbf{X}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}f_{0}=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}+\boldsymbol{\pi}:\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{h}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}+(\nabla_{j}\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j})\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\cdot\nabla_{i}(\partial_{j}\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{h}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}+\nabla_{j}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\cdot\left[\nabla_{i}(\partial_{j}\mathbf{p})-\nabla_{j}(\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{h}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}+\nabla_{j}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})~,

(269)

where one has used that
\boldsymbol{\pi}=\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}
, the definition of the total molecular field
\mathbf{h}
(Eq.
38
), the definition of the gradient of polarity
185
and the commutation relation Eq.
194
.
This relation reflects the property that the free energy density
f_{0}
is a function only of the surface internal variables, and leads to the relation given in Eq.
V.2
.

F.1.2
Invariance by rotation

We now discuss the invariance of free energy under an infinitesimal rotation
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
. Under this rotation the polar vector, curvature tensor, and gradient of polarity tensors transform as:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{p}~,

(270)

\displaystyle d\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{C}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{C}~,

(271)

\displaystyle d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})~,

(272)

which correspond to the requirements of a vanishing corotational variation
D\mathbf{p}=0
,
D\mathbf{C}=0
,
D(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})=0
. The concentration fields do not change,
dc^{\alpha}=0
. One then obtains for the change of free energy density under this rotation:

\displaystyle df_{0}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{C})+\mathbf{K}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{C})-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\pi}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p}))+\boldsymbol{\pi}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\cdot\left(\mathbf{C}\times_{1}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{C}\times_{2}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{h}_{0}\times\mathbf{p}-\boldsymbol{\pi}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\boldsymbol{\pi}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\cdot(\mathbf{C}\times_{1}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{C}\times_{2}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{h}_{0}\times\mathbf{p}-\epsilon_{i}{}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial_{j}\mathbf{p})\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\times\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})~.

(273)

where we have
used that
\boldsymbol{\pi}=\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}
, and used the notation for cross-products of tensors introduced in Eq.
158
and the related circular shift relation of triple products, Eqs.
159
and
160
. Invariance of the free energy density under a local rotation then leads to the following condition:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\times\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}+\epsilon_{i}{}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial_{j}\mathbf{p})\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{h}_{0}\times\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{C}\times_{1}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{C}\times_{2}\mathbf{K}~.

(274)

which is reported in the main text in Eq.
41
.
This equation can be projected on the normal and tangent directions to give the conditions:

\displaystyle 2C_{i}{}^{k}\epsilon_{k}{}^{j}K^{i}{}_{j}+(\mathbf{h}_{0}\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{n}-\epsilon_{i}{}^{j}\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial_{j}\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle-(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\times\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=0

(275)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\times\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{j}

\displaystyle=0

(276)

where one has used the symmetry of the tensor
K_{ij}
, and that
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{h}_{0}
are tangent to the surface.

F.2
Nematic surface

F.2.1
Invariance by translation

We now consider a nematic surface with free energy density
f_{0}(c^{\alpha},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{Q},\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})
with
\mathbf{Q}
the nematic tensor on the surface. One then obtains the gradient of the free energy density, using that it does not depend on the surface position:

\displaystyle\partial_{i}f_{0}=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{H}_{0}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{H}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}+(\nabla_{j}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}):\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}:\nabla_{i}\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{H}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}+\nabla_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}:(\nabla_{i}\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}-\nabla_{j}\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{i}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{H}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}+\nabla_{j}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q})~,

(277)

where one has used that
\boldsymbol{\Pi}=\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}
, the definition of the total molecular field
\mathbf{H}
(Eq.
94
) and the commutation relation Eq.
195
. Rearranging then gives the Gibbs-Duhem relation, Eq.
VI.2
.

F.2.2
Invariance by rotation

We proceed as in Appendix
F.1.2
and consider the effect of a local infinitesimal rotation
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
on the free energy density
f_{0}
. The curvature tensor transforms as in Eq.
271
under a rotation, and the nematic tensor and its gradient transform as

\displaystyle d\mathbf{C}=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{C}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{C}~,

(278)

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Q}=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}~,

(279)

\displaystyle d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})~,

(280)

which correspond to
D\mathbf{C}=0
,
D\mathbf{Q}=0
and
D(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})=0
. Here we use the notation for cross-product of tensors introduced in Eq.
A
. Under the infinitesimal rotation one also has
dc^{\alpha}=0
.
This then leads to the change of free energy density under the rotation:

\displaystyle df_{0}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{C})+\mathbf{K}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{C})-\mathbf{H}_{0}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle-\mathbf{H}_{0}:(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q}))

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q}))+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{3}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\cdot(\mathbf{C}\times_{1}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{C}\times_{2}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{i}{}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q})\mathbf{n}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}\times_{1}\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}\times_{2}\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q})~,

(281)

where we have used that
\boldsymbol{\Pi}=\mathbf{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}
, and used the notation for cross-products of tensors introduced in Eq.
158
and the related circular shift relation of triple products, Eqs.
159
and
160
. Invariance of the free energy density under this rotation then leads to the relation:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}\times_{1}\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{k}\times_{2}\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}+\epsilon_{i}{}^{j}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q})\mathbf{n}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{C}\times_{1}\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{C}\times_{2}\mathbf{K}~,

(282)

which is reported in Eq.
VI.2
.

Appendix G
Equilibrium forces

G.1
Generic equilibrium polar fluid membrane

G.1.1
Internal free energy

Here we calculate the free energy change associated to an infinitesimal surface shape change, using the infinitesimal variation of the surface free energy density given in Eq.
35
. We consider a shape change of the surface, such that a point at
\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})
is displaced to
\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})+d\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})
. We allow for an arbitrary change in
\mathbf{p}
and in the concentration
c^{\alpha}
. Calculating the variation of the free energy about equilibrium, we have

\displaystyle dF_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left(f_{0}\,g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+df_{0}\right)~,

(283)

where we have used the variation of the square root of the metric determinant, Eq.
207
.
The first term arises from area variation and the second from variation of
f_{0}
. Using the expression for the free energy density differential
df_{0}
, this can be rewritten

\displaystyle dF_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot d\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{K}:d\mathbf{C}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\pi}:d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]~,

(284)

where the variation of concentration that does not arise from dilution,
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}
, has been defined in Eq.
209
. This expression can be further rewritten,

\displaystyle dF_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[t_{e}^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}-\mathbf{h}\cdot D\mathbf{p}+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot D\mathbf{p}~,

(285)

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{e}^{i}=

\displaystyle\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-K^{ik}C^{j}{}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial^{j}\mathbf{p}\right]\mathbf{e}_{j}

\displaystyle+t_{e,n}^{i}\mathbf{n}~,

(286)

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}=

\displaystyle K^{ij}\epsilon_{j}{}^{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}+\mathbf{p}\times\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}~.

(287)

We postpone to the end of this section the calculation showing the equality between Eqs.
G.1.1
and
285
. We first note that using the expression for the variation of the free energy, eq.
42
, and the virtual work expression Eq.
265
,
\mathbf{t}_{e}^{i}
and
\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}
are indeed the equilibrium tension and moment tensors.

To obtain an explicit expression for the normal part of the equilibrium tension tensor
t^{i}_{e,n}
, we use the tangential torque balance condition Eq.
25
. We find:

\displaystyle t_{e,n}^{i}=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\bar{m}_{e}^{ji}+C_{j}{}^{k}m_{n,e}^{j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}-\epsilon^{ik}\nabla_{j}[\left(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C_{j}{}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j}\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{n}+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}-\epsilon^{ik}[\left(\nabla_{j}\boldsymbol{\pi}^{j})\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right]+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}+\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}\left[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{{\rm ext}}-\mathbf{h}\times\mathbf{p}\right]\cdot\mathbf{e}^{j}~,

(288)

where we have used the relation coming from invariance by rotation, Eq.
274
, and in the last line that
\mathbf{h}_{0}\times\mathbf{p}
is normal to the surface.

We now establish the equality between Eqs.
G.1.1
and
285
. The infinitesimal variation of vectors
\mathbf{a}
and second-rank tensors
\mathbf{B}
can be decomposed in a corotational part and a infinitesimal rotation arising from the surface rotation
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}
,
d\mathbf{a}=D\mathbf{a}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{a}
and
d\mathbf{B}=D\mathbf{B}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{B}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{B}
(Eqs
1
and
3
). A local infinitesimal rotation of the surface and its associated vector and tensor fields does not change the free energy density of the surface. This property has been used in Appendix
F
to obtain a Gibbs-Duhem relation. Such a local rotation is equivalent to performing a rotation of the vectors
\mathbf{a}
and tensors
\mathbf{B}
together with the shape rotation, such that
D\mathbf{a}=0
and
D\mathbf{B}=0
. Therefore, only corotational variations of vectors and tensor lead to a change in the surface free energy, and one can rewrite Eq.
G.1.1
:

\displaystyle dF_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot D\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{K}:D\mathbf{C}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\pi}:D(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[((f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial^{j}\mathbf{p})\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot D\mathbf{p}+K^{ij}DC_{ij}+\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}D\mathbf{p}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{p})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[((f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial^{j}\mathbf{p})\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{h}\cdot D\mathbf{p}+K^{ij}\left(-\frac{dg_{ik}}{2}C^{k}{}_{j}+\epsilon_{j}{}^{k}\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\theta}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\cdot(\mathbf{p}\times\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot D\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[((f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot\partial^{j}\mathbf{p}-K^{ik}C_{k}{}^{j})\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{h}\cdot D\mathbf{p}+(K^{ik}\epsilon_{k}{}^{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}+\mathbf{p}\times\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i})\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\,\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot D\mathbf{p}~.

(289)

To go from the first to the second line above, we have used
Eq.
222
. To go from the second to the third line, we have performed an integration by parts and have used the definition

\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{h}_{0}+\nabla_{i}\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}
. We have also used Eq.
214
to reexpress the differential of the curvature tensor in terms of
(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\theta})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{j}
and the variations of the metric tensor.

G.1.2
External forces and torques arising from an external potential

We consider here forces arising from an external potential
U=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dSc^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})
, where
u^{\alpha}
acts on component
\alpha
. Variation of the surface shape
d\mathbf{X}
and of the polarity vector
\mathbf{p}
leads to

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot d\mathbf{X}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\cdot(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{n})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\cdot\left(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{p}\right)+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\cdot D\mathbf{p}\right]+u^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]\,,

(290)

where we have used Eq.
209
for
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}
, a change of concentration not arising from dilution;
d\mathbf{n}=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{n}
; and Eq.
1
, giving here
d\mathbf{p}=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{p}+D\mathbf{p}
.
The expression for
dU
can then be rewritten

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot d\mathbf{X}-\left(\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\times\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\times\mathbf{p}\right)\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\cdot D\mathbf{p}\right]+u^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]\,.

(291)

Comparing this expression to the external virtual work
dW_{\rm ext}
defined in Eq.
28
, and using the expression for the variation of the external potential
43
, one obtains expressions for the equilibrium external force density, torque density, molecular field, and external chemical potentials:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}

\displaystyle=-c^{\alpha}\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}~,

(292)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}_{e}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}\left(\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\times\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\times\mathbf{p}\right)~,

(293)

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}~,

(294)

\displaystyle\mu^{\rm ext,\alpha}

\displaystyle=-u^{\alpha}~.

(295)

With these definitions, the variation of external potential can be written

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[-\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot d\mathbf{X}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}\cdot D\mathbf{p}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mu^{\rm ext,\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]~.

(296)

As a result of these equilibrium equations, the normal torque can be written:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{e,n}^{\rm ext}=(\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{n}~,

(297)

and we also note the following identity:

\displaystyle c^{\alpha}\partial_{i}\mu^{{\rm ext},\alpha}=

\displaystyle-c^{\alpha}\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{p}}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{e,i}^{\rm ext}+C_{ij}\epsilon^{jk}\left[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{e}^{\rm ext}-\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}\times\mathbf{p}\right]\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{p}~.

(298)

G.2
Generic equilibrium nematic fluid membrane

G.2.1
Internal free energy

We now consider a nematic fluid membrane with free energy for a region of surface
\mathcal{S}
given by
F_{0}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dSf_{0}
and free energy differential given by Eq.
91
. The surface is also subjected to an external potential
U=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dSc^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{n},\mathbf{Q})
, where
u^{\alpha}
is an external potential density acting on species
\alpha
. We consider a shape change of the surface, displacing
\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})
to
\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})+d\mathbf{X}(s^{1},s^{2})
.

Subjected to a variation of the surface shape, of the concentration field
c^{\alpha}
and of
\mathbf{Q}
, the variation of the free energy about equilibrium is

\displaystyle dF_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left(f_{0}\,g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+df_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}-\mathbf{H}_{0}:d\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{K}:d\mathbf{C}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]~,

(299)

where the variation of concentration that does not arise from dilution,
{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}
, has been defined in Eq.
209
. This expression can be rewritten:

\displaystyle dF_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[t_{e}^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}+\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}-\mathbf{H}:D\mathbf{Q}+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\,\nu_{i}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:D\mathbf{Q}~,

(300)

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{e}^{i}

\displaystyle=\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-K^{ik}C_{k}{}^{j}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial^{j}\mathbf{Q}\right]\mathbf{e}_{j}

\displaystyle+t_{e,n}^{i}\mathbf{n}~,

(301)

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}

\displaystyle=K^{ik}\epsilon_{k}{}^{j}\mathbf{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}~.

(302)

As for the polar case, we show the equality of Eq.
G.2.1
and
300
at the end of this section. We first note that using the expression for the variation of the free energy
98
and the virtual work expression Eq.
265
,
\mathbf{t}_{e}^{i}
and
\mathbf{m}_{e}^{i}
are indeed the equilibrium tension and moment tensors.

To obtain an explicit expression for the normal part of the equilibrium tension tensor
t^{i}_{e,n}
, we use the tangential torque balance condition Eq.
25
. We find:

\displaystyle t_{e,n}^{i}=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}\bar{m}_{e}^{ji}+C_{j}{}^{k}m_{n,e}^{j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}-\epsilon^{ik}\nabla_{j}[\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C_{j}{}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{n}+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}-\epsilon^{ik}\left[((\nabla_{j}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j})\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+(\nabla_{j}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{j})\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle+\Gamma^{{\rm ext},j}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}K^{ji}+\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}\left[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{{\rm ext}}-(\mathbf{H}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{H}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})\right]\cdot\mathbf{e}^{j}~,

(303)

where we have used the relation coming from invariance by rotation, Eq.
F.2.2
; and in the last line that the vectors
\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{H}_{0}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}
are normal to the surface.

We now establish the equality between Eqs.
G.2.1
and
300
. Starting from Eq.
G.2.1
, we obtain:

\displaystyle dF_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}-\mathbf{H}_{0}:D\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{K}:D\mathbf{C}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\Pi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}D(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{Q})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[\left((f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial^{j}\mathbf{Q}\right)\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{H}_{0}:D\mathbf{Q}+K^{ij}DC_{ij}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial_{i}D\mathbf{Q}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q})+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[\left((f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial^{j}\mathbf{Q}\right)\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{H}:D\mathbf{Q}+K^{ij}\left(-\frac{dg_{ik}}{2}C^{k}{}_{j}+\epsilon_{j}{}^{k}\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\theta}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\cdot(\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\,\nu_{i}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:D\mathbf{Q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[\left(\left(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})g^{ij}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:\partial^{j}\mathbf{Q}-K^{ik}C_{k}{}^{j}\right)\right)\frac{dg_{ij}}{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(K^{ij}\epsilon_{j}{}^{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q})\cdot\partial_{i}d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{H}:D\mathbf{Q}+\mu^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\,\nu_{i}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:D\mathbf{Q}\,.

(304)

In the first line, we have used the invariance of the free energy density by solid rotation (Appendix
F.2.2
).
To go from the first to the second line, we have used
Eq.
223
.
To go from the second to the third line, we have performed an integration by parts, we have used the definition for the total molecular field

\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}_{0}+\nabla_{i}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}
, Eq.
214
and the triple product relations
160
.

G.2.2
Forces and torques arising from an external potential

We consider here forces arising from an external potential
U=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dSc^{\alpha}u^{\alpha}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{n},\mathbf{Q})
, where
u^{\alpha}
acts on component
\alpha
. Variation of the surface shape
d\mathbf{X}
and of the second-rank tensor
\mathbf{Q}
leads to

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot d\mathbf{X}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\cdot(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times\mathbf{n})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}:\left(d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}\right)+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}:D\mathbf{Q}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+u^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]

(305)

where we have used Eq.
3
, giving here
d\mathbf{Q}=d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}+D\mathbf{Q}
, and the variation of the normal vector, Eq.
213
. The expression for
dU
can then be rewritten

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot d\mathbf{X}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}:D\mathbf{Q}\right]+u^{\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-c^{\alpha}\left(\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\times\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}\right)\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\right]\,

(306)

where we have used the triple product relations
160
.
Then, comparing this expression to the external virtual work
dW_{\rm ext}
defined in Eq.
28
, and using the equilibrium relation Eq.
99
, one obtains expressions for the equilibrium external force density, torque density, molecular field, and external chemical potentials:

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}

\displaystyle=-c^{\alpha}\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}

(307)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}_{e}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}\left(\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\times\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}\right)

(308)

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}

\displaystyle=c^{\alpha}\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}

(309)

\displaystyle\mu^{\rm ext,\alpha}

\displaystyle=-u^{\alpha}~.

(310)

With these definitions, the variation of external potential can be written

\displaystyle dU=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[-\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot d\mathbf{X}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}\cdot d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}+\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}:D\mathbf{Q}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mu^{\rm ext,\alpha}{\rm d}\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006ptc^{\alpha}\right]~.

(311)

We note that since
\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}
and
\mathbf{Q}
are symmetric and
\mathbf{Q}
is a tangent tensor, the normal torque can be written:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}^{\rm ext}=

\displaystyle[\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}+\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}]\cdot\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{i}{}^{k}H^{{\rm ext},ij}Q_{kj}\,.

(312)

We also note the following identity:

\displaystyle c^{\alpha}\partial_{i}\mu^{{\rm ext},\alpha}=

\displaystyle-c^{\alpha}\partial_{i}u^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-c^{\alpha}\left[\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{X}}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{n}}\cdot\partial_{i}\mathbf{n}+\frac{\partial u^{\alpha}}{\partial\mathbf{Q}}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{e,i}^{\rm ext}+C_{ij}\epsilon^{jk}\left[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{e}^{\rm ext}-\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}\times_{1}\mathbf{Q}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}\times_{2}\mathbf{Q}\right]\cdot\mathbf{e}_{k}-\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}:\partial_{i}\mathbf{Q}~.

(313)

Appendix H
Entropy production rate

H.1
Entropy production rate for a polar fluid surface

In this Appendix we calculate the entropy production rate of a polar surface using a Lagrangian approach. That is, we follow a moving fluid surface element labeled by its center of mass (
s^{1},s^{2}
), moving with velocity
\mathbf{v}
. Expressions for the gradient of flow
v_{ij}
, the vorticity
\boldsymbol{\omega}
, the corotational of the curvature tensor
D_{t}C_{ij}
can then found from the replacements
d\mathbf{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{v}dt
,
dg_{ij}\rightarrow 2v_{ij}dt
,
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\omega}dt
,
DC_{ij}\rightarrow D_{t}C_{ij}dt
in Eqs.
206
,
210
, and
221
. Corresponding expressions are reported in Eqs.
60
-
V.4
. Including the external potential
U
, the total free energy is

\displaystyle F_{\rm tot}=F+U=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,(f_{\rm kin}+f_{0}+c^{\alpha}u^{\alpha})~,

(314)

where
f_{\mathrm{kin}}=\rho\,v^{2}/2
is the kinetic energy density. The differential of
f_{0}
is given in Eq.
35
:

df_{0}=\mu^{\alpha}dc^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:d\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot d\mathbf{p}+\boldsymbol{\pi}:d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})\,.\\

(315)

We consider the time derivative of the free energy,
\dot{F}=\dot{F}_{0}+\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}
, and first
calculate
\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}
:

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,f_{\mathrm{kin}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[\frac{\rho v^{2}}{2}\,v_{i}{}^{i}+\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left(\frac{\rho\,v^{2}}{2}\right)\right]\,,

(316)

where
{\rm d}/{\rm d}t
denotes a Lagrangian time derivative (Eq.
227
) and where we have used Eq.
207
. In the Lagrangian approach,

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left(\frac{\rho\,v^{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{2}}{2}\frac{{\rm d}\rho}{{\rm d}t}+\rho\mathbf{v}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{v}}{{\rm d}t}=-\frac{v^{2}}{2}\rho\,v_{i}{}^{i}+\mathbf{v}\cdot\rho\mathbf{a}\,,

(317)

where we have used the mass conservation equation:

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}\rho}{{\rm d}t}+\rho v_{i}{}^{i}=0~,

(318)

to go from the first to the second line. We thus obtain

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\rho\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{v}~.

(319)

We can express the first term in the integral above in terms of forces and moments by noting that, since we are taking a Lagrangian approach, we may make the replacements
d\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{v}\,dt
,
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\to\boldsymbol{\omega}\,dt
,
dg_{ij}/2\to v_{ij}dt
, and
DC_{ij}\to D_{t}C_{ij}dt
in the equality between Eqs.
27
and
30
. We thereby obtain

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[-\overline{t}^{ij}v_{ij}-\overline{m}^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}-m_{n}^{i}\omega_{in}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{ext}}\cdot\mathbf{v}+\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{ext}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\right]+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\left[\mathbf{t}^{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}+\mathbf{m}^{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\right]\,.

(320)

with
\omega_{in}=(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{\omega})\cdot\mathbf{n}
.

We next calculate
\dot{F}_{0}=d/dt\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,f_{0}
. Using the differential of
f_{0}
, Eq.
35
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[f_{0}v_{i}{}^{i}+\sum_{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}\partial_{t}c^{\alpha}+\mathbf{K}:\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{C}}{{\rm d}t}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{p}}{{\rm d}t}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\pi}:\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\nabla\otimes\mathbf{p})\right]\,.

(321)

The second term on the right-hand side can be re-written using mass conservation for species
\alpha
, Eq.
256
in a Lagrangian form, that is,

\frac{{\rm d}c^{\alpha}}{{\rm d}t}+c^{\alpha}v_{i}{}^{i}=r^{\alpha}-\nabla_{i}j^{\alpha,i}\,,

(322)

where
r^{\alpha}
is the chemical reaction rate at which a molecule of species is produced, and
j^{\alpha,i}
is the relative flux of
\alpha
. As a result,

\mu^{\alpha}\frac{{\rm d}c^{\alpha}}{{\rm d}t}=-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha}v_{i}{}^{i}+r^{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}+j^{\alpha,i}\partial_{i}\mu^{\alpha}-\nabla_{i}(\mu^{\alpha}j^{\alpha,i})\,,

(323)

Using the balance equations for tangential and normal fluxes, Eq.
257
, and for chemical reactions
258
, we then have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}\frac{{\rm d}c^{\alpha}}{{\rm d}t}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}[-v_{i}{}^{i}\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha}+r^{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha=2}^{N}[j^{\alpha,i}\partial_{i}\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}-\nabla_{i}(\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}j^{\alpha,i})]\,,

(324)

where

\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}=\mu^{\alpha}-\frac{m^{\alpha}}{m^{1}}\mu^{1}

(325)

is the relative chemical potential of species
\alpha
.

With implicit summation from
\alpha=1
to
N
for terms with
\mu^{\alpha}
, and from
\alpha=2
to
N
for terms with
\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}
, we therefore have

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\left[(f_{0}-\mu^{\alpha}c^{\alpha})v_{i}{}^{i}+\mathbf{K}:\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{C}}{{\rm d}t}-\mathbf{h}_{0}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{p}}{{\rm d}t}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\boldsymbol{\pi}:\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\nabla\otimes\mathbf{p})+j^{\alpha,i}\partial_{i}\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}+r^{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}\right]

\displaystyle-\oint\nu_{i}dl\,\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}j^{\alpha,i}\,.

(326)

The remaining terms in Eq.
321
can be transformed using the results from section
G.1
by using the equality of Eqs.
G.1.1
and
285
, with the replacements
dg_{ij}/2\to v_{ij}dt
,
d\mathbf{p}\to\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{p}}{{\rm d}t}dt
,
d\mathbf{C}\to\frac{{\rm d}\mathbf{C}}{{\rm d}t}dt
,
d(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\otimes\mathbf{p})\rightarrow\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}(\nabla\otimes\mathbf{p})dt
,
d\boldsymbol{\vartheta}\to\boldsymbol{\omega}\,dt
and
D\mathbf{p}\rightarrow D_{t}\mathbf{p}dt
:

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\Big[\overline{t}_{e}^{ij}v_{ij}+\overline{m}_{e}^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}-\mathbf{h}\cdot D_{t}\mathbf{p}+m_{n,e}^{i}\omega_{in}

\displaystyle+j^{\alpha,i}\partial_{i}\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}+r^{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}\Big]+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}\left(-\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}j^{\alpha,i}+\boldsymbol{\pi}^{i}\cdot D_{t}\mathbf{p}\right)\,.

Finally, we calculate the time derivative of the external potential
U
, using Eq.
G.1.2
and Eq.
322
:

\displaystyle\dot{U}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[-\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{v}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}+\mathbf{h}^{\rm ext}\cdot D_{t}\mathbf{p}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mu^{{\rm ext},\alpha}r^{\alpha}+\overline{\mu}^{{\rm ext},\alpha}\nabla_{i}j^{\alpha,i}\right]\,,

(327)

where

\overline{\mu}^{{\rm ext},\alpha}=\mu^{{\rm ext},\alpha}-\frac{m^{\alpha}}{m^{1}}\mu^{{\rm ext},1}

(328)

is the external relative chemical potential.
Putting together
\dot{F}_{\mathrm{kin}}
,
\dot{F}_{0}
and
\dot{U}
, we finally obtain Eq.
59
.

H.2
Entropy production rate for a nematic fluid surface

We now calculate the entropy production rate for a nematic surface.
We carry over much of the derivation from the previous section, in particular the calculation of
\dot{F}_{\rm kin}
is unchanged. In the calculation of
\dot{F}_{0}
, we use the differential of the free energy introduced in Eq.
91
.
We also use the equality between Eq.
G.2.1
and eq.
300
. We then obtain:

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{0}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\,\Big[\overline{t}_{e}^{ij}v_{ij}+\overline{m}_{e}^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}-\mathbf{H}:D_{t}\mathbf{Q}+m_{n,e}^{i}\omega_{in}

\displaystyle+r^{\alpha}\mu^{\alpha}+j^{\alpha,i}\partial_{i}\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}\Big]+\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dl\nu_{i}(-\overline{\mu}^{\alpha}j^{\alpha,i}+\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{i}:D_{t}\mathbf{Q})\,.

(329)

The time derivative of the external potential
U
reads, using Eq.
G.2.2
:

\displaystyle\dot{U}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}dS\left[-\mathbf{f}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot\mathbf{v}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\rm ext}_{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}+\mathbf{H}^{\rm ext}:D_{t}\mathbf{Q}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mu^{{\rm ext},\alpha}r^{\alpha}+\overline{\mu}^{{\rm ext},\alpha}\nabla_{i}j^{\alpha,i}\right]\,,

(330)

where the relative external chemical potential
\bar{\mu}^{{\rm ext},\alpha}
is defined in Eq.
328
.

We can then compute
\dot{F}_{\rm tot}=\dot{F}+\dot{U}=\dot{F}_{0}+\dot{F}_{\rm kin}+\dot{U}
with the aid of Eqs.
320
,
329
and
H.2
, obtaining Eq.
VI.4
.

Appendix I
Full constitutive equations

Here we give complete constitutive equations, including terms for isotropic surfaces which do not couple to the polar or nematic order parameter on the surface. We include terms which are first-order in the curvature tensor
C_{ij}
, first order in polarity vector
\mathbf{p}
and its associated nematic tensor
q_{ij}=p_{i}p_{j}-\frac{1}{2}p^{2}g_{ij}
for polar surfaces and first order in the nematic tensor
Q_{ij}
for nematic surfaces. We do not include cross-coupling terms in the gradient of the chemical potential,
\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}
or the flux
j^{\alpha,i}
, except for active couplings with the fuel hydrolysis chemical potential,
\Delta\mu
. For simplicity, “viscous” coupling coefficients between
\bar{t}^{ij}_{d}
,
\bar{m}^{ij}
,
m_{n,d}^{i}
and
v_{ij}
,
D_{t}C_{ij}
,
\omega_{in}
which depend on the curvature, polarity vector or nematic tensor are not considered. The same simplification is applied to “diagonal” coupling coefficients between a flux and its conjugate force. We do not include couplings between the mechanical tensors and the deviatoric molecular field, and conversely between
D_{t}\mathbf{p}
or
D_{t}\mathbf{Q}
and
v_{ij}
,
D_{t}C_{ij}
,
\omega_{in}
, that depend on the curvature tensor.

I.1
Polar surface

The tension tensor has contributions:

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{0}=

\displaystyle 2\eta\tilde{v}^{ij}+\eta_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\zeta\Delta\mu g^{ij}+\zeta_{\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{ij}

\displaystyle+\frac{\nu}{2}(p^{i}h_{d}^{j}+p^{j}h_{d}^{i}-h_{d,k}p^{k}g^{ij})+\nu^{\prime}h_{d,k}p^{k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\eta}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\bar{\eta}_{b}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+2\tilde{\zeta}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\zeta^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{C}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{C}}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{PC}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{PC}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)+\nu_{\rm PC}^{\prime}\epsilon_{kl}p^{k}h_{d}^{l}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\frac{\nu_{{\rm PC}}}{2}(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}h_{d}^{k}p^{j}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}h_{d}^{k}p^{i}-\epsilon_{lk}h_{d}^{k}p^{l}g^{ij})

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm PC{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}.

(331)

The bending moment tensor reads

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{0}=

\displaystyle 2\eta_{c}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\eta_{cb}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+2\tilde{\zeta_{c}}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}+\zeta_{c}^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{c\rm n}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}q^{ij}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\eta}\tilde{v}^{ij}+\bar{\eta}_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\frac{\beta}{2}(p^{i}h_{d}^{j}+p^{j}h_{d}^{i}-p_{k}h_{d}^{k}g^{ij})

\displaystyle+\beta^{\prime}p_{k}h_{d}^{k}g^{ij}+\zeta_{c}\Delta\mu g^{ij}+\zeta_{c\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{ij}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle-\eta_{\rm{C}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)+\beta_{\rm{C}}^{\prime}\epsilon^{k}{}_{l}p_{k}h_{d}^{l}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta_{\rm{C}}}{2}(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}h_{d}^{k}p^{j}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}h_{d}^{k}p^{i}-\epsilon_{lk}h_{d}^{k}p^{l}g^{ij})

\displaystyle+\zeta_{c{\rm C}{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{PC}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{cPC}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{c\rm{PCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm{PCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{i})~.

(332)

Contributions to the tensor
m_{n}
read:

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n0}=

\displaystyle\kappa\omega^{i}_{n}+\chi_{\rm p}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}\Delta\mu+\psi\,\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}h_{d}^{j}

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle(\overline{\chi}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\overline{\chi}^{\prime}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle(\chi_{\rm{C}p}C^{ij}+\chi_{\rm{C}p}^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{PC}}=

\displaystyle\kappa_{\rm{PC}}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{jn}+\chi_{\rm{PC}\rm{p}}p^{i}\Delta\mu+\psi_{\rm PC}h_{d}^{i}~.

(333)

with
\omega_{in}=\partial_{i}\omega_{n}-C^{ij}\omega_{j}
.
Terms contributing to the dynamics of the polarity field read:

\displaystyle P_{0}^{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}h_{d}^{i}-\nu\tilde{v}^{ij}p_{j}-\nu^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}p^{i}+\lambda\Delta\mu p^{i}+\psi\,\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{jn}

\displaystyle P_{\rm UD}^{i}=

\displaystyle-\beta p_{j}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\beta^{\prime}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}p^{i}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm UD}\Delta\mu\,C^{ij}p_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{\rm UD}\Delta\mu\,C^{j}{}_{j}p^{i}

\displaystyle P_{\rm C}^{i}=

\displaystyle\beta_{\rm{C}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{kj}p_{j}+\beta_{\rm{C}}^{\prime}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{\rm C}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\lambda_{\rm C}^{\prime}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle P_{\rm PC}^{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}h_{d}^{j}+\nu_{\rm{PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}\tilde{v}^{kj}p_{j}+\nu_{\rm{PC}}^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}-\psi_{\rm PC}\omega^{i}_{n}\,.

(334)

The flux of species
\alpha=2...N
, relative to the centre of mass has different contributions given by

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{0}=

\displaystyle-L^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}+\kappa_{\rm p}^{\alpha}p^{i}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle(\kappa_{\rm{UD}p}^{\alpha}C^{ij}+\kappa_{\rm{UD}p}^{\prime\alpha}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle(\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\prime\alpha}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm PC}=

\displaystyle-L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}+\kappa_{\rm PCp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}\Delta\mu~,

(335)

where we have used the relation
\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}=\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}+C_{k}{}^{k}\epsilon^{ij}
to avoid introducing redundant couplings in the equation for
\mathbf{j}_{C}
.

Finally, the rate of fuel consumption has the following decomposition:

\displaystyle r_{0}=

\displaystyle-\zeta v_{k}{}^{k}-2\tilde{\zeta}_{c}\tilde{C}^{ij}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\zeta^{\prime}_{c}C_{k}{}^{k}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}-\zeta_{\rm n}v^{ij}q_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm n}q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{c\rm n}C_{k}{}^{k}q^{ij})D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-\chi_{\rm p}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}\omega_{in}+\lambda p_{i}h_{d}^{i}-\kappa_{\rm p}^{\alpha}p^{i}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}+\Lambda\Delta\mu

\displaystyle r_{\rm UD}

\displaystyle=-\zeta^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}v_{k}{}^{k}-2\tilde{\zeta}\tilde{C}^{ij}v_{ij}-\zeta_{c}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}^{\prime}q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k})v_{ij}-\zeta_{\rm cn}q^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\overline{\chi}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\overline{\chi}^{\prime}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\omega_{in}

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{\rm UD}C^{ij}+\lambda^{\prime}_{\rm UD}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{j}h_{d,i}

\displaystyle-(\kappa_{\rm UDp}^{\alpha}C^{ij}+\kappa_{\rm UDp}^{\prime\alpha}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{j}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle r_{\rm C}=

\displaystyle-2\zeta_{\rm C}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}v^{i}{}_{j}-(2\zeta_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{kl}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j})v_{ij}

\displaystyle-\zeta_{\rm cCn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\chi_{\rm Cp}C^{ij}+\chi_{\rm Cp}^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{j}\omega_{in}

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{\rm C}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\lambda_{\rm C}^{\prime}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}h_{d,i}

\displaystyle-(\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+{\kappa^{\prime}}_{\rm Cp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle r_{\rm PC}=

\displaystyle-2\zeta_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}D_{t}C^{i}{}_{j}-\zeta_{\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}v_{ij}

\displaystyle-(2\zeta_{\rm cPCn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm cPCn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j})D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-\chi_{\rm PCp}p^{i}\omega_{in}+\lambda_{\rm PC}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}h_{d,i}-\kappa_{\rm PCp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{ij}p_{j}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}.

(336)

The odd or Hall viscosities
\eta_{\rm PC}
,
\eta_{c{\rm PC}}
,
\kappa_{\rm PC}
, the transport coefficients
L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}
and the inverse rotational viscosity
1/\gamma_{\rm PC}
are odd under time-reversal; they can be non-zero for a system close to equilibrium for instance in the presence of a magnetic field
avron1998odd
. They can emerge more generally as a linear response coefficient around a non-equilibrium steady-state
banerjee2017odd
.

I.2
Pseudopolar surface

For a pseudopolar surface described by the order parameter
\mathbf{p}_{\rm C}
or by the combination
\epsilon_{\rm PC}
,
\mathbf{p}_{\rm C}
, the constitutive equations read:

\displaystyle\overline{t}_{d}^{ij}=

\displaystyle 2\eta\tilde{v}^{ij}+\eta_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\zeta\Delta\mu g^{ij}+\nu^{\prime}h_{{\rm C},d}^{k}p_{{\rm C},k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm n}\Delta\mu q^{ij}+\frac{\nu}{2}(p_{\rm C}^{i}h_{{\rm C},d}^{j}+p_{\rm C}^{j}h_{{\rm C},d}^{i}-h_{{\rm C},d}^{k}p_{{\rm C},k}g^{ij})

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\eta_{\rm{PC}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)+\zeta_{\rm PC{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\nu_{{\rm PC}}}{2}(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}h^{k}_{{\rm C},d}p_{\rm C}^{j}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}h_{{\rm C},d}^{k}p_{\rm C}^{i}-\epsilon_{kl}h_{{\rm C},d}^{l}p_{\rm C}^{k}g^{ij})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\nu_{\rm PC}^{\prime}\epsilon_{kl}p_{\rm C}^{k}h_{{\rm C},d}^{l}g^{ij}\right]

(337)

\displaystyle\overline{m}_{d}^{ij}=

\displaystyle 2\eta_{c}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\eta_{cb}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+2\tilde{\zeta_{c}}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}+\zeta_{c}^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{c{\rm n}}\Delta\mu q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}_{c{\rm n}}}\Delta\mu q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\eta_{\rm{cPC}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta_{\rm{cPCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm{cPCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{i})\right]

(338)

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n,d}

\displaystyle=\kappa\omega^{i}_{n}+(\chi_{\rm{C}p}C^{i}{}_{j}+\chi_{\rm{C}p}^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{i}{}_{j})p_{\rm C}^{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}(\kappa_{\rm{PC}}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{jn}+(\overline{\chi}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{ik}C_{kj}+\overline{\chi}^{\prime}_{\rm p}\epsilon_{jk}C^{ki})p_{\rm C}^{j}\Delta\mu)

(339)

\displaystyle D_{t}p_{\rm C}^{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}h_{{\rm C},d}^{i}-\nu\tilde{v}^{ij}p_{{\rm C},j}-\nu^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}p_{\rm C}^{i}+\lambda\Delta\mu p_{\rm C}^{i}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}h_{{\rm C},d}^{j}+\nu_{\rm{PC}}\epsilon^{ik}\tilde{v}_{kj}p_{\rm C}^{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\nu_{\rm{PC}}^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}\epsilon^{ij}p_{{\rm C},j}+\lambda_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{i}{}_{j}p_{\rm C}^{j}\right]

(340)

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}=

\displaystyle-L^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}+(\kappa^{\alpha}_{\rm Cp}\epsilon^{ik}C_{kj}+\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\prime\alpha}\epsilon_{jk}C^{ki})p_{\rm C}^{j}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{\rm PC}L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}(\kappa^{\alpha}_{\rm{UD}p}C^{i}{}_{j}+\kappa_{\rm{UD}p}^{\prime\alpha}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{i}{}_{j})p_{\rm C}^{j}\Delta\mu

(341)

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle-\zeta v_{k}{}^{k}-\zeta_{\rm n}v^{ij}q_{ij}-2\tilde{\zeta}_{c}\tilde{C}^{ij}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\zeta^{\prime}_{c}C_{k}{}^{k}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{c{\rm n}}q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{c\rm n}C_{k}{}^{k}q^{ij})D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\chi_{\rm Cp}C^{ij}+\chi_{\rm Cp}^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{i}{}_{j})p_{\rm C}^{j}\omega_{in}+\Lambda\Delta\mu

\displaystyle+\lambda p_{{\rm C},i}h_{{\rm C},d}^{i}-(\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\alpha}\epsilon^{ik}C_{kj}+\kappa_{\rm Cp}^{\prime\alpha}\epsilon_{jk}C^{ki})p_{\rm C}^{j}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[-\lambda_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ij}p_{\rm C}^{j}h_{{\rm C},d}^{i}+2\zeta_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}D_{t}C^{i}{}_{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta_{\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{j}v_{ij}+(\overline{\chi}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\overline{\chi}^{\prime}_{\rm p}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})p_{j}\omega_{in}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\kappa^{\alpha}_{\rm UDp}C^{ij}+\kappa_{\rm UDp}^{\prime\alpha}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij})p_{{\rm C},j}\partial_{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\alpha}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(2\zeta_{c\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}q_{l}{}^{j})D_{t}C_{ij}\right]

(342)

I.3
Nematic surface

The contributions to the tension tensor read:

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{0}=

\displaystyle 2\eta\tilde{v}^{ij}+\eta_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\zeta\Delta\mu g^{ij}+\nu H_{d}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\nu^{\prime}Q_{kl}H_{d}^{kl}g^{ij}+\zeta_{\rm n}\Delta\mu Q^{ij}

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\eta}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\bar{\eta}_{b}(D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k})g^{ij}+2\tilde{\zeta}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\zeta^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta_{\rm n}}\Delta\mu Q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}_{\rm n}}\Delta\mu Q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{C}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{C}}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}Q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{\rm{PC}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{PC}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)+\nu_{\rm PC}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}H_{d}^{kj}

\displaystyle+\nu_{\rm PC}^{\prime}\epsilon^{kl}Q_{km}H_{d}^{ml}g^{ij}+\zeta_{\rm PC{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}.

(343)

The contributions to the moment tensor, in the decomposition indicated in Eq.
70
, read

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{0}=

\displaystyle 2\eta_{c}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\eta_{cb}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+2\tilde{\zeta_{c}}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}+\zeta_{c}^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{c{\rm n}}\Delta\mu Q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}_{c{\rm n}}}\Delta\mu Q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\eta}\tilde{v}^{ij}+\bar{\eta}_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\beta H_{d}^{ij}+\beta^{\prime}Q_{kl}H_{d}^{kl}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\zeta_{c}\Delta\mu g^{ij}+\zeta_{c{\rm n}}\Delta\mu Q^{ij}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle-\eta_{\rm{C}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)+\beta_{\rm C}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}H_{d}^{kj}

\displaystyle+\beta_{\rm C}^{\prime}\epsilon^{kl}Q_{km}H_{d}^{ml}g^{ij}+\zeta_{c{\rm C}{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{\rm{PC}}=

\displaystyle\eta_{\rm{cPC}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{c\rm{PCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}Q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{i})

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm{PCn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{i})~.

(344)

Contributions to the tensor
m_{n}
read:

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n0}

\displaystyle=\kappa\omega_{in}

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{UD}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{C}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle m^{i}_{n\rm{PC}}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\rm{PC}}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{jn}

(345)

and the corotational time derivative of the nematic order parameter is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}^{ij}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}H_{d}^{ij}-\nu\tilde{v}^{ij}-\nu^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}Q^{ij}+\lambda\Delta\mu\,Q^{ij}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\rm UD}^{ij}=

\displaystyle-\beta D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\beta^{\prime}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}Q^{ij}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm UD}\Delta\mu\,\tilde{C}^{ij}+\lambda_{\rm UD}^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}Q^{ij}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\rm C}^{ij}=

\displaystyle\beta_{\rm C}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{kj}+\beta_{\rm C}^{\prime}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q^{kj}D_{t}C_{l}{}^{l}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm C}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})+\lambda_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\rm PC}^{ij}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}H_{d}^{kj}+\nu_{\rm PC}\epsilon^{ik}\tilde{v}_{k}{}^{j}

\displaystyle+\nu^{\prime}_{{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q^{kj}v_{l}{}^{l}+\lambda_{\rm PC}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}~.

(346)

The flux of species
\alpha=2...N
, relative to the centre of mass has different contributions given by

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{0}=

\displaystyle-L^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm{UD}}=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm{C}}=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{\rm PC}=

\displaystyle-L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}~.

(347)

Finally, the rate of fuel consumption has the following decomposition:

\displaystyle r_{0}=

\displaystyle-\zeta v_{k}{}^{k}-2\tilde{\zeta}_{c}\tilde{C}^{ij}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\zeta^{\prime}_{c}C_{k}{}^{k}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}-\zeta_{\rm n}v^{ij}Q_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{c{\rm n}}Q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{\rm cn}C_{k}{}^{k}Q^{ij})D_{t}C_{ij}+\lambda Q_{ij}H_{d}^{ij}+\Lambda\Delta\mu

\displaystyle r_{\rm UD}=

\displaystyle-\zeta^{\prime}C_{k}{}^{k}v_{k}{}^{k}-2\tilde{\zeta}\tilde{C}^{ij}v_{ij}-\zeta_{c}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}Q^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}^{\prime}Q^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k})v_{ij}-\zeta_{c{\rm n}}Q^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle+(\lambda_{\rm UD}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\lambda^{\prime}_{\rm UD}C_{k}{}^{k}Q^{ij}){H_{d}}_{ij}

\displaystyle r_{\rm C}=

\displaystyle-2\zeta_{\rm C}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}v^{i}{}_{j}-(2\zeta_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{kl}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{j})v_{ij}

\displaystyle-\zeta_{\rm cCn}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}D_{t}C_{ij}+2\lambda_{\rm C}\epsilon_{jk}C^{k}{}_{i}H_{d}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm{Cn}}C_{k}{}^{k}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}{H_{d}}_{ij}

\displaystyle r_{\rm PC}=

\displaystyle-2\zeta_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}D_{t}C^{i}{}_{j}-\zeta_{\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{j}v_{ij}

\displaystyle-(2\zeta_{c\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}Q_{k}{}^{l}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{c\rm PCn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{k}{}^{l}Q_{l}{}^{j})D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}Q^{k}{}_{j}H_{d}^{ij}.

(348)

As for the polar case, the odd or Hall viscosities
\eta_{\rm PC}
,
\eta_{c{\rm PC}}
,
\kappa_{\rm PC}
, the transport coefficients
L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}
and the inverse rotational viscosity
1/\gamma_{\rm PC}
are odd under time-reversal, and can be non-zero in a system close to equilibrium for instance in the presence of a magnetic field.

I.4
Pseudonematic surface

For pseudonematic surfaces whose broken symmetries can be described by the pseudotensor
\mathbf{Q}_{\rm UD}
and the combination of the pseudoscalar and pseudovector
\{\epsilon_{\rm PC},\mathbf{Q}_{\rm UD}\}
, we find:

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}_{d}=

\displaystyle 2\eta\tilde{v}^{ij}+\eta_{b}v_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\zeta g^{ij}\Delta\mu

\displaystyle+\nu^{\prime}Q_{{\rm UD},kl}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}\Delta\mu Q_{\rm UD}^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\zeta}^{\prime}_{\rm n}\Delta\mu Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k}+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\eta_{\rm{PC}}\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}v^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}v^{ki}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\nu_{\rm PC}^{\prime}\epsilon^{kl}Q_{{\rm UD},km}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{ml}g^{ij}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kl}C_{l}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kl}C_{l}{}^{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm{Cn}}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{ik}C_{kl}Q_{\rm UD}^{lj}+\epsilon^{jk}C_{kl}Q_{\rm UD}^{li})\right]

(349)

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{ij}_{d}=

\displaystyle 2\eta_{c}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}+\eta_{cb}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+2\tilde{\zeta_{c}}\Delta\mu\tilde{C}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\zeta_{c}^{\prime}\Delta\mu C_{k}{}^{k}g^{ij}+\beta H_{{\rm UD}d}^{ij}+\zeta_{c{\rm n}}\Delta\mu Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\eta_{\rm{cPC}}\left(\epsilon^{ik}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{j}+\epsilon^{jk}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{i}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta_{\rm{PC}\Delta\mu\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}\right)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\beta_{\rm C}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{kj}+\zeta_{c{\rm C}{\rm n}}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kj}\right]

(350)

\displaystyle m_{n,d}^{i}=

\displaystyle\kappa\omega_{n}^{i}+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\kappa_{\rm PC}\epsilon^{ij}\omega_{jn}

(351)

\displaystyle D_{t}Q^{ij}_{\rm UD}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{ij}+\lambda\Delta\mu Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}+\lambda_{\rm UD}\Delta\mu\,\tilde{C}^{ij}

\displaystyle-\nu^{\prime}v_{k}{}^{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}-\beta D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{kj}+\nu^{\prime}_{{\rm PC}}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kj}v_{l}{}^{l}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\rm C}\,\left(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}D_{t}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}D_{t}C^{ki}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\lambda_{\rm C}\Delta\mu(\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}C^{kj}+\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki})+\lambda_{\rm PC}\Delta\mu\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kj}\right]

(352)

\displaystyle j^{\alpha,i}_{0}=

\displaystyle-L^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{i}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}-\epsilon_{\rm PC}L_{\rm PC}^{\alpha\beta}\epsilon^{ij}\partial_{j}\bar{\mu}_{d}^{\beta}~

(353)

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\Lambda\Delta\mu-\zeta v_{k}{}^{k}-2\tilde{\zeta}_{c}\tilde{C}^{ij}D_{t}\tilde{C}^{ij}-\zeta^{\prime}_{c}C_{k}{}^{k}D_{t}C_{k}{}^{k}

\displaystyle+\lambda Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}H_{{\rm UD}d,ij}+\lambda_{\rm UD}\tilde{C}_{ij}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{ij}-\zeta_{c\rm n}Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}D_{t}C_{ij}

\displaystyle-(\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}Q_{\rm UD}^{kl}C_{kl}g^{ij}+\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm n}^{\prime}Q_{\rm UD}^{ij}C_{k}{}^{k})v_{ij}

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\rm PC}\left[-2\zeta_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}C^{kj}D_{t}C^{i}{}_{j}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(2\zeta_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kl}C_{l}{}^{j}+2\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm Cn}\epsilon^{ik}C_{kl}Q_{\rm UD}^{lj})v_{ij}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\zeta_{\rm cCn}\epsilon^{i}{}_{k}Q_{\rm UD}^{kj}D_{t}C_{ij}+2\lambda_{\rm C}\epsilon^{j}{}_{k}C^{ki}H_{{\rm UD}d,ij}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\lambda_{\rm PC}\epsilon_{ik}{Q_{\rm UD}}^{k}{}_{j}H_{{\rm UD}d}^{ij}\right]~.

(354)

Appendix J
Glossary of physical realisations of surface symmetries

In this Appendix we give examples of physical realisations of surfaces with symmetries that are discussed in the main text (see Tables
2
,
4
,
6
and Fig.
3
). We discuss mostly examples of biological membranes bilayers and epithelia.

•

D_{\rm\infty h}^{\perp}
,
isotropic
:
Corresponds to the symmetry of pure phospholipid membrane bilayers used for instance in giant unilamellar vesicles
walde2010giant
. Another example is given by free standing smectic
A
films
degennesprost
.

•

C_{\rm\infty v}^{\perp}
,
isotropic, up-down broken symmetry
:
Corresponds to the symmetry of a phospholipid bilayer in which the two leaflets have a different composition. An alternative is provided by a conventional phospholipid bilayer loaded with ion pumps
manneville1999activity
, oriented in a preferred direction.

•

D_{\infty}^{\perp}
,
isotropic, chiral broken symmetry
:
Corresponds to the symmetry of a phospholipid-cholesterol membrane bilayer
veatch2003separation
, a smectic
A
free standing film made of chiral molecules, or a free standing cell monolayer film with no apico-basal polarity (since cells are chiral
inaki2016cell
).

•

C_{\rm\infty h}^{\perp}
,
isotropic, planar- chiral broken symmetry
:
Such a symmetry may be obtained with spin polarised molecules or magnetically polarised nanoparticles, with magnetic polarisation along the membrane normal, embedded in a conventional phospholipid membrane. An alternative way to obtain such a symmetry would be to have an isotropic bilayer, one leaflet with an excess of dextrorotatory chiral molecules, the other with an excess of levorotatory ones, in exactly the opposite concentration. An out of equilibrium version would be obtained by a membrane composed of rotary motors with rotation axis parallel to the membrane normal and with up-down symmetry, embedded in a conventional phospholipid membrane. Note that motors such as F
0
F
1
-ATPase do not have up-down symmetry
noji1997direct
.

•

C_{\infty}^{\perp}
,
isotropic, chiral and up-down broken symmetries
:
Most biological membranes correspond to this symmetry group. They contain chiral molecules such as cholesterol, oriented ion pumps, different leaflet composition, and are in-plane isotropic.

•

C_{2\rm v}^{||}
,
polar
:
A simple example is that of a membrane with polar order parallel to the membrane. We do not know of such a system made of small molecules, neither do we know of more complex realisations.

•

C_{\rm s}^{||}
,
polar, up-down broken symmetry
:
This corresponds to a polar phase with polarity parallel to the membrane with broken up-down symmetry. The simplest example is that of a tilted phospholipid monolayer. Such phases are known in Langmuir films
adamson1967physical
;
kaganer1999structure
. An example at a cell level would be a membrane, on one side of which actin polymerization takes place with a tilt with respect to the membrane normal. The composite membrane-cortex layer has the required symmetry if we neglect actin chirality. At the tissue scale this phase corresponds to any polar epithelial tissue, either
in vivo
or
in vitro
on a substrate, the polarity having components both along and perpendicular to the membrane, provided we again neglect the weak chirality of cells. Such tissues have a spontaneous tendency to move homogeneously with respect to their substrate
jain2020role
.

•

C_{2}^{||}
,
polar, chiral broken symmetry
:
This phase typically corresponds to a free standing chiral smectic
C
film
degennesprost
or a phospholipid bilayer made of chiral molecules that are tilted with respect to the normal. Such phases have not been described in biological membranes, but could exist.

•

C_{\rm s}^{\perp}
,
polar, planar-chiral broken symmetry
: A possible picture is that of a polar membrane, with polarity parallel to the membrane, embedding rotatory motors with up-down symmetry and rotation axis parallel to the membrane normal.

•

C_{1}
,
polar, chiral and up-down broken symmetry
:
Adding chirality to any of the physical realizations of
C_{s}^{||}
membranes suppresses mirror planes, and leads to membrane which are not invariant under any point group symmetry operation. This is, for example, the case of tilted chiral phospholipid bilayers with inequivalent leaflets, as well as epithelium with in-plane polar symmetry and apico-basal polarity, since they lack up-down symmetry and cells are chiral. On the cell surface, the actomyosin cortex, with polar and chiral actin filaments polymerizing away from the cell membrane, enters this category
salbreux2012actin
.

•

C_{2\rm h}^{||}
,
pseudopolar
:
The simplest physical picture is that of a free standing smectic
C
film
degennesprost
, or a phospholipid bilayer membrane in which the molecules are tilted with respect to the membrane normal. An alternative out-of-equilibrium version would correspond to a similar membrane in which rotatory motors with up-down symmetry and rotation axis parallel to the membrane are embedded in the membrane.

•

C_{i}=S_{2}
,
pseudopolar and planar-chiral broken symmetry
:
This phase corresponds to a
C_{2\rm h}^{||}
, pseudopolar surface, with an additional broken symmetry. We are not aware of an experimental realisation of such a surface.

•

D_{\rm 2h}^{\perp}
,
D_{2h}^{||}
,
nematic
:
This phase corresponds to a membrane with in-plane nematic order. To our knowledge there is no simple example with phospholipids or smectic free standing films. Thin spherical nematic shells obtained with a nematic drop containing a smaller aqueous drop would enter this category
fernandez2007novel
;
lopez2011frustrated
.

•

C_{\rm 2v}^{\perp}
,
nematic, up-down broken symmetry
:
A simple representation is of a membrane with nematic order parallel to the membrane and with no up-down symmetry. Such a membrane type, not reported to our knowledge in systems made of small molecules, corresponds to many epithelial layers
in vivo
or
in vitro
when they rest on a substrate and cells spontaneously elongate, whenever cell chirality can be neglected
duclos2017topological
;
duclos2018spontaneous
.

•

D_{2}^{\perp}
,
D_{2}^{||}
,
nematic, chiral broken symmetry
:
Corresponds to a membrane made of chiral molecules with in-plane nematic order. We do not know of any example with phospholipids or free standing smectic films.

•

C_{\rm 2h}^{\perp}
,
nematic, planar-chiral broken symmetry
:
A simple realization would be obtained by dissolving planar pinwheel-shaped molecules in an otherwise nematic membrane with nematic order parallel to the membrane. We know of no such realization.

•

C_{2}^{\perp}
,
nematic, chiral and up-down broken symmetries
:
This corresponds to an in-plane nematic order, with chiral molecules and broken up-down symmetry. We do not know of such phases made of phospholipids.
Chiral microtubule filaments with no coarse-grained polarity but orientational order coated on a lipid vesicle
keber2014topology
, or nematic epithelial layers on substrate or
in vivo
provide good examples of such phases
duclos2017topological
;
duclos2018spontaneous
.

•

D_{\rm 2d}^{\perp}
,
pseudonematic
:
Corresponds to a bilayer membrane in which a nematic order parallel to the membrane exists in each monolayer, but with their main axis being orthogonal to each other. We do not know of any example of such a phase made of small molecules. The supracellular actin fiber system in
Hydra
could provide a beautiful example of such a symmetry, if we ignore chirality and assume that the orthogonal actin network fibers have an equal degree of organisation
Maroudas-Sacks:2021aa
.

•

S_{4}^{\perp}
,
pseudonematic and planar-chiral broken symmetry:

We are not aware of an experimental realisation of such a surface.

Appendix K
Quasi one-dimensional confined active film

Here we describe calculations for the steady-state solutions of a confined, quasi-dimensional active film. We assume that the system has invariance of translation in the
y
direction, is confined along the
x
direction, and is free from external forces and torques. The active film position in space
\mathbf{X}(x,y)
is denoted using the Monge gauge:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(x,y)=x\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{x}+y\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{y}+h(x,y)\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{z}

(355)

with
h
an height function, and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{x}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{y}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{z}
are unit vectors of a 3D cartesian basis. We assume a weakly deformed surface,
|\partial_{x}h|\ll 1
and
|\partial_{y}h|\ll 1
. To first order in the height function gradients,
g_{ij}\simeq\delta_{ij}
, the Christoffel coefficients vanish and
C_{ij}\simeq-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}h
. Because
g_{ij}\simeq\delta_{ij}
, we use indifferently contravariant and covariant tensor components. At low Reynolds number where inertial terms can be neglected, the relevant force balance equations (Eqs.
23
-
26
) then are, in the weakly deformed limit,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}t_{xx}+C_{xx}\partial_{x}\bar{m}_{xx}

\displaystyle=0

(356)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}t_{xy}

\displaystyle=0

(357)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{2}\bar{m}_{xx}-C_{xx}t_{xx}

\displaystyle=0~.

(358)

In both examples discussed below we assume that
t_{xy}(0)=t_{xy}(L)=0
(no stress tangent to the active film at the boundary), resulting in
t_{xy}=0
.

Using the normal torque balance Eq.
26
, the total tension tensor can be written:

\displaystyle t^{ij}=

\displaystyle t^{ij}_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ij}(\nabla_{k}m_{n}^{k}-C_{kl}m^{kl})~.

(359)

In this section we consider only equilibrium contributions to
m_{n}^{k}
. Using Eq.
135
, one finds

\displaystyle\nabla_{k}m_{n}^{k}=\nabla_{k}m_{n,e}^{k}=\epsilon_{kl}p^{k}h^{l}-K\epsilon_{kl}p^{k}p^{j}C_{ij}C^{il}~.

(360)

Using Eqs.
359
-
360
and the relation between the symmetric part of the tension tensor
t_{s}^{ij}
and
\bar{t}^{ij}
, Eq.
31
; one obtains:

\displaystyle t^{ij}=

\displaystyle\overline{t}^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{ij}\left[\epsilon_{kl}p^{k}h^{l}-K\epsilon^{km}p_{k}p_{l}C^{nl}C_{nm}\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(\overline{m}^{ik}C_{k}{}^{j}+\overline{m}^{jk}C_{k}{}^{i}-C_{kl}\bar{m}^{km}\epsilon_{m}{}^{l}\epsilon^{ij}\right)~.

(361)

To find the total tension tensor (Eqs.
136
and
146
), the expression of the modified equilibrium tension tensor, which follows from Eqs.
31
,
134
and
135
, is useful:

\displaystyle\bar{t}^{ij}_{e}=

\displaystyle\left[\gamma+\frac{\kappa}{2}(C_{k}{}^{k})^{2}+\frac{K}{2}(\partial_{i}\mathbf{p})^{2}\right]g^{ij}-K(\nabla^{i}p^{k})(\nabla^{j}p_{k})~.

(362)

Using Eq.
133
, the tangential components of the total molecular field have explicit expression:

\displaystyle h^{i}=-\lambda_{p}p^{i}+K\left(\nabla_{j}\nabla^{j}p^{i}-C^{ij}C_{jk}p^{k}\right)~,

(363)

which gives with the simplifications considered here:

\displaystyle h_{x}=-\lambda_{p}p_{x}+K\partial_{x}^{2}p_{x}~,

(364)

\displaystyle h_{y}=-\lambda_{p}p_{y}+K\partial_{x}^{2}p_{y}~.

(365)

K.1
Flat planar chiral active film

We first discuss a flat planar film confined between two walls, such that
v_{x}(x=0)=v_{x}(x=L)=0
. Since we consider an incompressible fluid, one has
\partial_{x}v_{x}+\partial_{y}v_{y}=0
. Translational invariance in the
y
direction then implies
\partial_{x}v_{x}=0
which then results in
v_{x}=0
.
The normal component of the vorticity
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\times\mathbf{v}
(defined in Eq.
61
) is
\omega_{n}=\partial_{x}v_{y}/2
. Using the constitutive equation tension tensor, Eq.
136
,
one obtains:

\displaystyle t_{xy}=

\displaystyle 2\eta\omega_{n}+\frac{\nu}{2}(p_{x}h_{y}+p_{y}h_{x})+\frac{\nu_{\rm PC}}{2}(h_{y}p_{y}-h_{x}p_{x})

\displaystyle+\zeta_{\rm n}\Delta\mu p_{x}p_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{{\rm PCn}}\Delta\mu(p_{y}^{2}-p_{x}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{x}h_{y}-p_{y}h_{x}\right)~.

(366)

Writing
p_{x}=\cos\theta
,
p_{y}=\sin\theta
, the polarity equation
137
results in an equation for the time evolution of the angle
\theta
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta=

\displaystyle\frac{K}{\gamma}\partial_{x}^{2}\theta+\omega_{n}(1-\nu\cos 2\theta-\nu_{\rm PC}\sin 2\theta)-\lambda_{\rm{PC}}\Delta\mu~,

(367)

and in an expression for the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
obtained by imposing
